<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that the arithmetic exponent of square matrix multiplication over every fixed field satisfies <i>ω</i> &lt; 2.371054886006746. This includes every positive characteristic.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Over every fixed field $\mathbb F$, the arithmetic exponent of square matrix multiplication satisfies $$\omega_\mathbb F<2.371054886006746<2.371054887.$$
  - Explanation: The arithmetic exponent bound holds over every fixed field, including positive characteristic.
- **C2** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-direct-sum)): Let $s,a,b,c$ be positive integers. If $$\operatorname{R}\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s}\langle a,b,c\rangle\right)\leq R_*,$$ then $$\label{eq:identical-direct-sum} s(abc)^{w/3}\leq R_*.$$
  - Explanation: A rank bound for a direct sum of identical oriented matrix tensors constrains its common volume and rank exponent.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-cw-rank)): Over every field and for every integer $m\geq0$, $$\label{eq:cw-power-rank} \operatorname{R}(\mathrm{CW}_q^{\otimes m}) \leq(q+2)^m\binom{3m+2}{2}.$$
  - Explanation: Laurent coefficient extraction bounds exact rank without interpolation or field division.
- **C4** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-joint-step)): Let $B_N$ be the restriction of $\bigotimes_r F_r^{\otimes m_r}$ by the inherited masks just specified, with the population data above. Set $$\label{eq:joint-totals} A_{\rm amb}=\sum_{r\in\mathcal R}c_rH(p_r),\qquad C_W=\sum_{r\in\mathcal R}c_rC_{r,W},\qquad E=\min\{C_X,C_Y,C_Z\}.$$ If $E>0$, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ one can choose positive output widths, as small as desired, such that for all sufficiently large $N$ divisible by the fixed type denominators, a direct sum of $\exp(o(N))$ independent copies of $B_N$ has a linear restriction to at least $\lfloor\exp(N(E-\varepsilon))\rfloor$ independent copies of $Q_N$.* *The choices of populations, rational laws, positive widths, and divisibility requirements precede the limit $N\to\infty$. In particular, the assertion does not require common strand lengths.
  - Explanation: Compatible priorities permit capacity pooling across heterogeneous populations and depths; fixed data and masks precede the strand limit.
- **C5** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-compatibility-count)): For fixed population data, there is a function $\xi(\eta,\chi)\to0$ as $(\eta,\chi)\to(0,0)$ such that any fine side variable passing the pair filters is compatible with at most $$\label{eq:compatibility-degree} \exp\bigl(N(A_{\rm amb}-C_W+\xi(\eta,\chi)) +O(\log N)\bigr),\qquad W\in\{Y,Z\},$$ target triples containing its coarse block, before hashing. The estimate is uniform in the variable and the target arrangement.
  - Explanation: An incidence count bounds the compatible target triples of a fine variable before hashing.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-orbit-masks)): The total number of $G_e$-orbits of variables on the three sides is polynomial in $N$, uniformly over target triples $e$. Suppose every inherited or global mask is either implied by the exact coarse choices, or tests an empirical ordered pair of child statistics (or a fixed function of that pair) against its prescribed mixture law with a fixed positive tolerance. Choose the child tolerances sufficiently smaller than those mask tolerances, with the mixture laws consistent as in Theorem <a href="#thm:joint-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:joint-step">4</a>. There is then a constant $C_0$ such that, in every full-variable orbit on every side, the fraction failing any of these deterministic masks is at most $C_0/N$. The constant may depend on the fixed data and tolerances, but not on $e$, the orbit, or $N$.
  - Explanation: Consistent inherited masks lose a vanishing fraction uniformly on complete-variable orbits with fixed data and tolerances.
- **C7** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hole-repair)): Let $Q_N$ be a tensor over an arbitrary field, with at most $\exp(bN)$ nonzero monomials for a fixed $b>0$. Let $G_N$ be a finite group of coefficient-preserving permutations of its variables, acting separately on the three sides. Suppose a variable restriction $H_N$ of $Q_N$ omits at most $C/N$ of each $G_N$-orbit of variables, for a fixed $C>0$. For all sufficiently large $N$, a direct sum of $$R_N=\exp\bigl(O(N/\log N)\bigr)$$ copies of $H_N$ has $Q_N$ as a linear restriction. Every coefficient is reconstructed exactly once. The bound is uniform over all $H_N,Q_N,G_N$ satisfying these same constants.
  - Explanation: Group shifts and presence-mask rectangles recover coefficients exactly using a subexponential replication bound.
- **C8** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-replication)): Suppose $D_{s_1}(T)\preceq D_{r_1}(S)$ and $D_{s_2}(V)\preceq D_{r_2}(T)$. Then $$D_{s_1s_2}(V)\preceq D_{r_1r_2}(S).$$ For any finite collection of tensors, $$\bigotimes_{i=1}^j D_{r_i}(F_i) \cong D_{\prod_{i=1}^j r_i}\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^jF_i\right).$$ The same assertions permit different maps in different direct-sum branches, provided a common upper bound on their replication counts is used. Consequently every fixed finite sequence of the joint extractions above, including finite tensor products of them, costs only $\exp(o(N))$ replications of its original input.
  - Explanation: Finite composition and tensor products preserve subexponential replication overhead.
- **C9** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-leaf-volume)): Fix a finite number $K$ of lots and rational stage laws satisfying the prescriptions of Section <a href="#sec:stagger" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stagger">5</a>. Use fixed rational subdivisions retaining each history class’s prescribed laws, as in <a href="#eq:subdivision-restriction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:subdivision-restriction">[eq:subdivision-restriction]</a>; exact capacity balance is not required for this volume count. For all sufficiently divisible $N$, every completed ideal output in the staggered construction has a restriction to the same oriented matrix tensor $\langle a_N,b_N,c_N\rangle$, with $$\log(a_Nb_Nc_N)\geq KN S_*-O_K(\log(N+2)). \label{eq:leaf-volume-bound}$$ The implied constant may depend on the fixed laws and subdivisions. For limiting real data, the corresponding volume rate can approach $S_*$ arbitrarily closely by consistent rational approximation.
  - Explanation: Exact history counts and terminal matching restrictions give common oriented dimensions and their logarithmic volume rate.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-staggered-yield)): Assume the prescriptions of Section <a href="#sec:stagger" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stagger">5</a>, the balance conditions <a href="#eq:balance-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:balance-feasibility">[eq:balance-feasibility]</a>, and positive entries of $L_A,L_B,L_C$. Let $S_*=S_0+S_1+S_2$ be the terminal rate defined above. For every fixed integer $K\geq2$ and every $\epsilon>0$, suitable fixed rational approximations, part allocations, and tolerances give, for all sufficiently large divisible $N$, a restriction of $\exp(o_K(N))$ copies of $\mathrm{CW}_5^{\otimes8KN}$ to $s_N$ identical matrix tensors of volume $V_N$, where $$\label{eq:finite-stagger-yield} \frac{\log s_N}{N}\geq K(H_0+C_*)-\beta-\epsilon, \qquad \frac{\log V_N}{N}\geq KS_*-\epsilon.$$
  - Explanation: A fixed finite staggered schedule yields identical matrix tensors with controlled boundary deficit and terminal volume.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-numerical-certificate)): The exact parameters above satisfy every positivity and balance condition used in Proposition <a href="#prop:staggered-yield" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:staggered-yield">10</a>. All thirty partial capacity entries in Table <a href="#tab:capacities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:capacities">1</a> are positive. In particular, $S_*>0$, $T\log2-B_*>0$, all entries of $L_A,L_B,L_C$ are positive, and the three balance fractions are strictly positive. Moreover, $$\begin{aligned} 5.612983956059236756&<H_0+C_*<5.612983956059236757,\label{eq:yield-certificate}\\ 12.594770321594676362&<S_*<12.594770321594676363,\label{eq:volume-certificate}\\ 2.371054886006745684 &<\frac{3(8\log7-H_0-C_*)}{S_*} <2.371054886006745685.\label{eq:ratio-certificate} \end{aligned}$$ With $\Omega=2.371056$, the strict comparison has the outward enclosure $$\label{eq:gap-certificate} 0.000004676829725968 < H_0+C_*+\frac{\Omega}{3}S_*-8\log7 <0.000004676829725969.$$
  - Explanation: Exact rational distributions and outward logarithm enclosures certify feasibility and the strict exponent comparison.

        "anchor": "stmt-thm-joint-step",
        "exact_quote": "Let $B_N$ be the restriction of $\\bigotimes_r F_r^{\\otimes m_r}$ by the inherited masks just specified, with the population data above. Set $$\\label{eq:joint-totals}\n A_{\\rm amb}=\\sum_{r\\in\\mathcal R}c_rH(p_r),\\qquad\n C_W=\\sum_{r\\in\\mathcal R}c_rC_{r,W},\\qquad\n E=\\min\\{C_X,C_Y,C_Z\\}.$$ If $E>0$, then for every $\\varepsilon>0$ one can choose positive output widths, as small as desired, such that for all sufficiently large $N$ divisible by the fixed type denominators, a direct sum of $\\exp(o(N))$ independent copies of $B_N$ has a linear restriction to at least $\\lfloor\\exp(N(E-\\varepsilon))\\rfloor$ independent copies of $Q_N$.*\n\n*The choices of populations, rational laws, positive widths, and divisibility requirements precede the limit $N\\to\\infty$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-orbit-masks",
        "exact_quote": "The total number of $G_e$-orbits of variables on the three sides is polynomial in $N$, uniformly over target triples $e$. Suppose every inherited or global mask is either implied by the exact coarse choices, or tests an empirical ordered pair of child statistics (or a fixed function of that pair) against its prescribed mixture law with a fixed positive tolerance. Choose the child tolerances sufficiently smaller than those mask tolerances, with the mixture laws consistent as in Theorem <a href=\"#thm:joint-step\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:joint-step\">4</a>. There is then a constant $C_0$ such that, in every full-variable orbit on every side, the fraction failing any of these deterministic masks is at most $C_0/N$. The constant may depend on the fixed data and tolerances, but not on $e$, the orbit, or $N$.",
        "source_id": "PM-107.3"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-hole-repair",
        "exact_quote": "Let $Q_N$ be a tensor over an arbitrary field, with at most $\\exp(bN)$ nonzero monomials for a fixed $b>0$. Let $G_N$ be a finite group of coefficient-preserving permutations of its variables, acting separately on the three sides. Suppose a variable restriction $H_N$ of $Q_N$ omits at most $C/N$ of each $G_N$-orbit of variables, for a fixed $C>0$. For all sufficiently large $N$, a direct sum of $$R_N=\\exp\\bigl(O(N/\\log N)\\bigr)$$ copies of $H_N$ has $Q_N$ as a linear restriction. Every coefficient is reconstructed exactly once. The bound is uniform over all $H_N,Q_N,G_N$ satisfying these same constants.",
        "source_id": "PM-107.3"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-replication",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose $D_{s_1}(T)\\preceq D_{r_1}(S)$ and $D_{s_2}(V)\\preceq D_{r_2}(T)$. Then $$D_{s_1s_2}(V)\\preceq D_{r_1r_2}(S).$$ For any finite collection of tensors, $$\\bigotimes_{i=1}^j D_{r_i}(F_i)\n \\cong D_{\\prod_{i=1}^j r_i}\\left(\\bigotimes_{i=1}^jF_i\\right).$$ The same assertions permit different maps in different direct-sum branches, provided a common upper bound on their replication counts is used. Consequently every fixed finite sequence of the joint extractions above, including finite tensor products of them, costs only $\\exp(o(N))$ replications of its original input.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Matrix multiplication exponent** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a fixed field $\mathbb F$, the matrix multiplication exponent $\omega=\omega_\mathbb F$ is the infimum of the real numbers $\tau$ for which two $n\times n$ matrices over $\mathbb F$ can be multiplied using $O(n^{\tau+o(1)})$ scalar additions and multiplications.
- **Tensor rank** ([source](#sec:foundations)): Its rank $\operatorname{R}(T)$ is the least number of products of three linear forms, one on each side, whose sum is $T$.
- **Restriction** ([source](#sec:foundations)): A *restriction* substitutes linear forms separately on the three sides.
- **Strand** ([source](#pm-h-10)): A *strand of length $\ell$* is a copy of $\mathrm{CW}_q^{\otimes\ell}$; its variable indices are words of length $\ell$.
- **Shape tensor** ([source](#pm-h-10)): For a triple $g=(g_0,g_1,g_2)$ of nonnegative integers with $g_0+g_1+g_2=2\ell$, let $T_{\ell,g}$ be the restriction in which the weight sums on the three sides are $g_0,g_1,g_2$.
- **Entropy** ([source](#sec:joint)): For a probability vector $p$ on a finite alphabet, write $H(p)=-\sum_a p(a)\log p(a)$, with $0\log0=0$.
- **Target triple** ([source](#pm-h-13)): A *target triple* $e$ is a triple of coarse sequences having exact joint type $p_r$ in each population.
- **Lot** ([source](#sec:stagger)): A *lot* consists of $N$ strands of length $8$, hence of $8N$ copies of $\mathrm{CW}_5$.
- **Variable restriction** ([source](#sec:recovery-exact)): A variable restriction of a tensor means that some basis variables are set to zero; all coefficients on the remaining variables retain their original values.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-numerical-certificate-2): The arithmetic exponent bound holds over every fixed field, including positive characteristic. -> The arithmetic exponent bound holds over every fixed field, including positive characteristic.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-direct-sum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-direct-sum): A rank bound for a direct sum of identical oriented matrix tensors constrains its common volume and rank exponent. -> A rank bound for a direct sum of identical oriented matrix tensors constrains its common volume and rank exponent.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-cw-rank) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cw-rank): Laurent coefficient extraction bounds exact rank without interpolation or field division. -> Laurent coefficient extraction bounds exact rank without interpolation or field division.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-thm-joint-step) / [proof](#sec:recovery-completion): Compatible priorities permit capacity pooling across heterogeneous populations and depths; fixed data and masks precede the strand limit. -> Compatible priorities permit capacity pooling across heterogeneous populations and depths; fixed data and masks precede the strand limit.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-compatibility-count) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-compatibility-count): An incidence count bounds the compatible target triples of a fine variable before hashing. -> An incidence count bounds the compatible target triples of a fine variable before hashing.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-lem-orbit-masks) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-orbit-masks): Consistent inherited masks lose a vanishing fraction uniformly on complete-variable orbits with fixed data and tolerances. -> Consistent inherited masks lose a vanishing fraction uniformly on complete-variable orbits with fixed data and tolerances.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-lem-hole-repair) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hole-repair): Group shifts and presence-mask rectangles recover coefficients exactly using a subexponential replication bound. -> Group shifts and presence-mask rectangles recover coefficients exactly using a subexponential replication bound.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-lem-replication) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-replication): Finite composition and tensor products preserve subexponential replication overhead. -> Finite composition and tensor products preserve subexponential replication overhead.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-prop-leaf-volume) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-leaf-volume): Exact history counts and terminal matching restrictions give common oriented dimensions and their logarithmic volume rate. -> Exact history counts and terminal matching restrictions give common oriented dimensions and their logarithmic volume rate.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-prop-staggered-yield) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-staggered-yield): A fixed finite staggered schedule yields identical matrix tensors with controlled boundary deficit and terminal volume. -> A fixed finite staggered schedule yields identical matrix tensors with controlled boundary deficit and terminal volume.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-prop-numerical-certificate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-numerical-certificate): Exact rational distributions and outward logarithm enclosures certify feasibility and the strict exponent comparison. -> Exact rational distributions and outward logarithm enclosures certify feasibility and the strict exponent comparison.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-leaf**: A zero-coordinate strand has a matrix dimension equal to the coefficient counting its matched words.
- **I-field**: The tensor identities and restrictions operate over the original field.
- **I-limits**: The strand-count limit precedes the lot-count limit.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-practical**: Treat the exponent bound as a practical runtime or bit-complexity guarantee. -> Arithmetic exponent bounds provide no practical crossover or bit-cost estimate here.
- **A-limits**: Grow the schedule together with the strand count using the same estimates. -> Uniform estimates for a growing schedule are not asserted.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The zero-coordinate strand formula counts a finite matching of complete words and gives an exact matrix dimension. The implementation covers this finite leaf count only.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON list of integer pairs [ell,j].
# PRECONDITIONS: 0 <= ell <= 8 and 0 <= j <= 2*ell; at most 100 pairs.
# OUTPUTS: JSON list of exact zero-coordinate leaf dimensions.
# TIME: O(sum(ell**2)) exact integer operations.
# SPACE: O(max(ell)) integer coefficients, excluding input and output.
# ARITHMETIC: python-flint fmpz; exact integers, no floating point.
# FAILURE: reject malformed or out-of-domain inputs with a nonzero exit.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpz
pairs = json.load(sys.stdin)
if not isinstance(pairs, list) or not 1 <= len(pairs) <= 100:
    raise ValueError("invalid batch")
answers = []
for pair in pairs:
    if not isinstance(pair, list) or len(pair) != 2:
        raise ValueError("invalid pair")
    ell, j = pair
    if type(ell) is not int or type(j) is not int or not (0 <= ell <= 8 and 0 <= j <= 2*ell):
        raise ValueError("outside domain")
    coeff = [fmpz(1)]
    for _ in range(ell):
        nxt = [fmpz(0) for _ in range(len(coeff)+2)]
        for k, value in enumerate(coeff):
            nxt[k] += value
            nxt[k+1] += 5 * value
            nxt[k+2] += value
        coeff = nxt
    answers.append(int(coeff[j]))
print(json.dumps(answers))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **fixed-field** (hypothesis): The arithmetic exponent is defined with the field fixed; constants may depend on the field and chosen finite construction. Blocked use: ['Uniform performance guarantees across varying fields.']
- **cost-model** (formal_scope): The theorem bounds asymptotic arithmetic cost. Bit-complexity and practical crossover estimates are ABSENT. Blocked use: ['A practical crossover prediction.', 'A bit-complexity bound.']
- **fixed-data** (hypothesis): Population masses, laws, alphabets and tolerances are fixed before the strand-count limit. Blocked use: ['Applying the joint step with populations or alphabets growing with the strand count.']
- **joint-positivity** (hypothesis): The joint extraction yield requires strictly positive minimum summed directional capacity and the fixed divisibility conditions. Blocked use: ['Using a nonpositive minimum capacity as a positive extraction guarantee.']
- **mask-scope** (hypothesis): Inherited masks must have the specified mixture centers and fixed positive widths; arbitrary inherited deletions are not covered. Blocked use: ['Repair guarantees for arbitrary inherited masks.']
- **identical-orientation** (hypothesis): The direct-sum rank bound requires identical oriented matrix dimensions among summands. Blocked use: ['Applying the identical-summand inequality to unrelated oriented dimensions.']
- **finite-repair** (nonconstructive): The recovery argument selects a covering family by a probabilistic existence argument; it gives no practical search-time bound for finding that family. Blocked use: ['A practical deterministic search algorithm with the stated replication overhead as its runtime.']
- **orbit-repair** (hypothesis): Repair assumes a coefficient-preserving permutation group and a missing-fraction bound on every variable orbit. Blocked use: ['Repair from a global average missing fraction without orbit control.']
- **limit-order** (formal_scope): The strand-count limit is taken for a fixed finite schedule; the lot-count limit follows afterwards. Blocked use: ['A uniform guarantee for simultaneously growing schedules.']
- **consistent-approximation** (approximation): Inherited mixtures must be defined from the same rational parameter choices, rather than approximated independently. Blocked use: ['Independent approximation of inherited mixture laws.']
- **feasibility-not-optimality** (formal_scope): The certificate establishes a feasible parameter choice without establishing optimality. Blocked use: ['A claim that the certified parameter choice is optimal.']
- **outward-certificate** (approximation): Numerical certification includes outward interval operations and the rational logarithm tail; decimal centers alone are insufficient. Blocked use: ['Treating rounded decimal centers as rigorous parameter certificates.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 107 (`lean/docs/107.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Complex Matrix Multiplication Below 2.258 and Rectangular Bounds](../../preprints/Complex-Matrix-Multiplication-Below-2.258-and-Rectangular-Bounds-September-24-2026/Complex-Matrix-Multiplication-Below-2.258-and-Rectangular-Bounds-September-24-2026.pdf)
> - [Staggered extraction for exact matrix multiplication over every field](../../preprints/Staggered-extraction-for-exact-matrix-multiplication-over-every-field-September-24-2026/Staggered-extraction-for-exact-matrix-multiplication-over-every-field-September-24-2026.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalized results bound the complex matrix-multiplication exponent by $\omega(\mathbb C)\le9/4$, the dual exponent by $\alpha>0.465$, and the rectangular exponent at aspect ratio $0.709$ by $\omega(\mathbb C;1,0.709,1)<2.092$. The dual exponent is the supremum of rectangular aspect ratios attainable with exponent $2$. The arithmetic model counts additions, subtractions, and multiplications in finite division-free programs, with arbitrary positive exponent slack. The square bound implies the paper's weaker $2.258$ headline bound.
>
> The formalized result gives the unconditional bound $\omega(F)<2.371054886006746$ for every field $F$, including finite fields and fields of positive characteristic. The exponent counts additions, subtractions, and multiplications in finite division-free arithmetic programs, with arbitrary positive exponent slack. No numerical inequalities remain as hypotheses. The result concerns arithmetic complexity, rather than bit complexity or practical crossover sizes.

Comparator source `MatrixFields.lean` (3293 bytes, sha256 `fe4f7ef409f69f7ef9542c6672e091c9cb6505e0d17c41cda01a2924c2729bc6`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

open scoped BigOperators Topology Polynomial

namespace MatrixAllFields

noncomputable section

open scoped BigOperators

namespace MatrixMultiplication.Arithmetic

inductive Gate (F Input Register : Type*) where
  | constant : F → Gate F Input Register
  | input : Input → Gate F Input Register
  | add : Register → Register → Gate F Input Register
  | sub : Register → Register → Gate F Input Register
  | mul : Register → Register → Gate F Input Register

namespace Gate

variable {F Input Register : Type*}

def eval [Field F] (inputs : Input → F) (registers : Register → F) :
    Gate F Input Register → F
  | .constant z => z
  | .input i => inputs i
  | .add i j => registers i + registers j
  | .sub i j => registers i - registers j
  | .mul i j => registers i * registers j

def cost : Gate F Input Register → ℕ
  | .constant _ => 0
  | .input _ => 0
  | .add _ _ => 1
  | .sub _ _ => 1
  | .mul _ _ => 1

end Gate

inductive Program (F Input : Type*) : ℕ → Type _ where
  | nil : Program F Input 0
  | step {r : ℕ} : Program F Input r → Gate F Input (Fin r) → Program F Input (r + 1)

namespace Program

variable {F Input : Type*}

def eval [Field F] : {r : ℕ} → Program F Input r → (Input → F) → Fin r → F
  | 0, .nil, _ => Fin.elim0
  | _ + 1, .step p g, inputs =>
    Fin.cases (g.eval inputs (p.eval inputs)) (p.eval inputs)

def cost : {r : ℕ} → Program F Input r → ℕ
  | 0, .nil => 0
  | _ + 1, .step p g => p.cost + g.cost

end Program

abbrev MatrixInput (a b c : ℕ) := (Fin a × Fin b) ⊕ (Fin b × Fin c)

def matrixInputs {F : Type*} {a b c : ℕ}
    (A : Matrix (Fin a) (Fin b) F) (B : Matrix (Fin b) (Fin c) F) :
    MatrixInput a b c → F
  | .inl (i, j) => A i j
  | .inr (j, k) => B j k

structure MatrixAlgorithm (F : Type*) (a b c : ℕ) where
  registers : ℕ
  program : Program F (MatrixInput a b c) registers
  output : Fin a → Fin c → Fin registers

namespace MatrixAlgorithm

variable {F : Type*} [Field F] {a b c : ℕ}

def eval (P : MatrixAlgorithm F a b c)
    (A : Matrix (Fin a) (Fin b) F) (B : Matrix (Fin b) (Fin c) F) :
    Matrix (Fin a) (Fin c) F :=
  fun i k => P.program.eval (matrixInputs A B) (P.output i k)

def Correct (P : MatrixAlgorithm F a b c) : Prop :=
  ∀ (A : Matrix (Fin a) (Fin b) F) (B : Matrix (Fin b) (Fin c) F), P.eval A B = A * B

def cost (P : MatrixAlgorithm F a b c) : ℕ := P.program.cost

end MatrixAlgorithm

/-- Positive slack, one uniform constant, and a correct program at every size. -/
def AdmissibleExponent (F : Type*) [Field F] (τ : ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ C : ℝ, 0 < C ∧
    ∀ n : ℕ, 1 ≤ n → ∃ P : MatrixAlgorithm F n n n,
      P.Correct ∧ (P.cost : ℝ) ≤ C * (n : ℝ) ^ (τ + ε)

def omega (F : Type*) [Field F] : ℝ := sInf {τ : ℝ | AdmissibleExponent F τ}

end MatrixMultiplication.Arithmetic

end

end MatrixAllFields

namespace MatrixAllFields.MatrixMultiplication.AllFieldMain

universe u

theorem omega_lt_source_constant (F : Type u) [Field F] :
    Arithmetic.omega F < (2371054886006746 : ℝ) / 10 ^ 15 ∧
    (2371054886006746 : ℝ) / 10 ^ 15 < (2371056 : ℝ) / 10 ^ 6 := by
  sorry

end MatrixAllFields.MatrixMultiplication.AllFieldMain

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that the arithmetic exponent of square matrix multiplication over every fixed field satisfies <i>ω</i> &lt; 2.371054886006746. This includes every positive characteristic.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| [stmt-lem-direct-sum](#stmt-lem-direct-sum) | lemma | `lem:direct-sum` | 2 | Let $s,a,b,c$ be positive integers. |
| [stmt-lem-cw-rank](#stmt-lem-cw-rank) | lemma | `lem:cw-rank` | 3 | Over every field and for every integer $m\geq0$, |
| [stmt-thm-joint-step](#stmt-thm-joint-step) | theorem | `thm:joint-step` | 4 | Let $B_N$ be the restriction of $\bigotimes_r F_r^{\otimes m_r}$ by the inherited masks just specified, with the population data above. |
| [stmt-lem-compatibility-count](#stmt-lem-compatibility-count) | lemma | `lem:compatibility-count` | 5 | For fixed population data, there is a function $\xi(\eta,\chi)\to0$ as $(\eta,\chi)\to(0,0)$ such that any fine side variable passing the pair filters is compatible with at most |
| [stmt-lem-orbit-masks](#stmt-lem-orbit-masks) | lemma | `lem:orbit-masks` | 6 | The total number of $G_e$-orbits of variables on the three sides is polynomial in $N$, uniformly over target triples $e$. |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For a fixed field $\mathbb F$, the matrix multiplication exponent $\omega=\omega_\mathbb F$ is the infimum of the real numbers $\tau$ for which two $n\times n$ matrices over $\mathbb F$ can be multiplied using $O(n^{\tau+o(1)})$ scalar additions and multiplications. This exponent measures the asymptotic arithmetic cost of a central bilinear operation and of many algorithms built from it. The field is fixed throughout; constants in an arithmetic algorithm may depend on the field and on its chosen finite construction.

Strassen’s seven-product algorithm for $2\times2$ matrices gave the subcubic exponent $\log_2 7$ (Strassen 1969) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Strassen1969](#ref-Strassen1969)]. Subsequent advances used tensors to record bilinear operations: tensor rank counts the products of linear forms needed to realize an operation. Approximate bilinear identities and their conversion to exact algorithms (Bini 1980) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Bini1980](#ref-Bini1980)], Schönhage’s asymptotic sum inequality (Schönhage 1981) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Schonhage1981](#ref-Schonhage1981)], and Strassen’s laser method (Strassen 1987) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Strassen1987](#ref-Strassen1987)] established the main ingredients of this approach. The sum inequality converts a low-rank direct sum of matrix tensors into an exponent bound. The laser method constructs such direct sums by restricting powers of a tensor with low asymptotic rank. Coppersmith and Winograd combined these ideas with progression-free sets and introduced the tensor family used here (Coppersmith and Winograd 1990) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[CoppersmithWinograd1990](#ref-CoppersmithWinograd1990)].

Passing to a larger tensor power gives more freedom in choosing the intermediate pieces before extracting matrix tensors from them. Stothers analyzed the fourth power, in work subsequently published with Davie (Stothers 2010; Davie and Stothers 2013) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Stothers2010](#ref-Stothers2010), [DavieStothers2013](#ref-DavieStothers2013)]. Vassilevska Williams analyzed the eighth power, and Le Gall continued through the thirty-second power (Vassilevska Williams 2012; Le Gall 2014) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[VassilevskaWilliams2012](#ref-VassilevskaWilliams2012), [LeGall2014](#ref-LeGall2014)]. Alman and Vassilevska Williams then reduced the extraction loss that arises when prescribed marginal distributions permit several joint distributions, obtaining $\omega<2.3728596$ (Alman and Vassilevska Williams 2024) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[AlmanWilliams2024](#ref-AlmanWilliams2024)].

A further source of loss is requiring entire variable blocks to be disjoint when the final matrix tensors need only disjoint subsets of those blocks. Duan, Wu, and Zhou used asymmetric hashing to reduce this *combination loss* in the recursive analysis (Duan, Wu, and Zhou 2023) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[DuanWuZhou2023](#ref-DuanWuZhou2023)]. Vassilevska Williams, Xu, Xu, and Zhou allowed finer sharing of variables and extended recovery from deletions on all three sides to the restricted tensor products needed in the recursion (Vassilevska Williams et al. 2023) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[VXXZ2023](#ref-VXXZ2023)]. Alman, Duan, Vassilevska Williams, Xu, Xu, and Zhou then introduced successive compatibility tests that treat all three sides differently (Alman et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[MoreAsymmetry2026](#ref-MoreAsymmetry2026)]. These tests underlie the extraction studied here.

For a recent numerical comparison, Dupont et al. obtained $\omega<2.371177$ by optimizing the eighth-power instance of this asymmetric framework, combining modern optimization with AlphaEvolve (Dupont et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Dupont2026](#ref-Dupont2026)]. Version 3 of *More Asymmetry Yields Faster Matrix Multiplication* records that improvement and credits the new parameters to Dupont et al. (Alman et al. 2026, Table 1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[MoreAsymmetry2026](#ref-MoreAsymmetry2026)]. We prove the following bound over every fixed field.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Over every fixed field $\mathbb F$, the arithmetic exponent of square matrix multiplication satisfies $$\omega_\mathbb F<2.371054886006746<2.371054887.$$*

</div>

The construction gives exact arithmetic algorithms, including in positive characteristic. Its finite choices are independent of the input matrices. The theorem concerns asymptotic arithmetic cost; the counting arguments and parameter choices do not give bit-complexity or practical crossover estimates.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Proof overview

We use the Coppersmith–Winograd tensor with parameter $5$, denoted $\mathrm{CW}_5$. Section <a href="#sec:foundations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:foundations">2</a> defines it and proves that the rank of its $m$th tensor power is at most $7^m$ times a polynomial in $m$, over the original field. A *strand of length $\ell$* is a copy of $\mathrm{CW}_5^{\otimes\ell}$. On each side the variables of $\mathrm{CW}_5$ are indexed by $0,\ldots,6$, with weights $0,1,1,1,1,1,2$. A strand variable is a word of these indices, and a *shape* records the weight sums on the three sides. We start with strands of length $8$ and split them successively into lengths $4$, $2$, and $1$. At each depth we restrict by shape. A shape with a zero coordinate already gives a matrix multiplication tensor; positive shapes pass to the next split.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Combining capacities across depths.

An extraction restricts a tensor to a direct sum of specified tensor products. To make the outputs disjoint, its three side-assignment tests each impose a bound on the guaranteed logarithmic number of outputs; we call these bounds its *directional capacities*. The guarantee uses their minimum. The constituent-stage analysis in (Alman et al. 2026, Theorem 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[MoreAsymmetry2026](#ref-MoreAsymmetry2026)] already combines differently prescribed populations at one depth before taking this minimum. Our joint step also combines populations at different depths, provided their priority orders agree on the physical tensor sides. For their weighted capacity vectors $v_r\in\mathbb R^3$, the benefit of pooling across depths is expressed by $$\sum_r\min_W v_{r,W}\ \leq\ \min_W\sum_r v_{r,W}.$$ Thus a shortage on one side at one depth can be offset by capacity from another depth.

Section <a href="#sec:joint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:joint">3</a> proves this joint extraction by following the actual order of the tests. On the first side, variables are grouped by their sequences of strand or half-strand weight sums; these groups are the *coarse blocks*. Each retained coarse block is assigned to a unique output. Compatibility with that assignment then determines which complete variables on the second and third sides can be retained. The result is a direct sum of the intended products with some variables deleted. Populations retain their ancestry and physical placement throughout, so each subsequent split receives its own prescribed distributions.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Recovering outputs with inherited restrictions.

Some variables are missing because an earlier extraction imposed type conditions, meaning conditions on empirical distributions of variable statistics. Others are deleted to resolve competing assignments in the current extraction. Earlier recovery arguments already treat three-side deletions and inherited type conditions (Vassilevska Williams et al. 2023, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[VXXZ2023](#ref-VXXZ2023)] <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[MoreAsymmetry2026](#ref-MoreAsymmetry2026)], (Alman et al. 2026, sec. 6.5). Section <a href="#sec:recovery" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:recovery">4</a> proves the form needed for our joint step. With $N$ the large strand-count parameter, the extraction selects outputs in which every orbit of complete variables under the allowed position permutations loses at most $C/N$ of its variables, uniformly over the orbits. Here $C$ depends only on the fixed population data and tolerances. Independent symmetry shifts then cover the ideal tensor. Partitioning variables by their presence in those shifts reconstructs every coefficient exactly once, over the original field. Only a subexponential number of independent input copies is needed. The recovered products can then be subdivided and used in further extractions.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Staggering and the final rank comparison.

A *lot* contains $N$ length-$8$ strands. We stagger finitely many lots so that, at an interior time step, one lot is at each of the three splitting stages. Stage priorities balance the summed directional capacities. The work is partitioned among the six physical axis orders; one joint extraction acts on each part, and the six output counts multiply. Section <a href="#sec:stagger" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stagger">5</a> constructs this schedule, shown in Figure <a href="#fig:pipeline" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:pipeline">1</a>, and computes its finite boundary loss. Section <a href="#sec:leaf-volumes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:leaf-volumes">6</a> counts the common oriented dimensions of the terminal matrix tensors and combines these counts with the schedule to prove the total yield and rank comparison.

<a id="eq:intro-constraint"></a>
Write $H_0$ for the initial logarithmic yield, $C_*$ for the contribution of the three staggered stages, and $S_*$ for the terminal logarithmic matrix volume, all normalized per initial length-$8$ strand. The direct-sum rank inequality then gives $$\label{eq:intro-constraint}
 \omega_\mathbb F\leq\frac{3(8\log7-H_0-C_*)}{S_*}.$$ For each fixed number of lots, we first take the divisible strand count $N$ to infinity and remove approximation losses; the number of lots tends to infinity last. Thus the boundary cost vanishes without a uniform assertion for a growing schedule. The explicit rational data in Section <a href="#sec:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:parameters">7</a> and Appendix <a href="#app:parameter-tables" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:parameter-tables">8</a> certify $S_*>0$ and place the ratio in <a href="#eq:intro-constraint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-constraint">[eq:intro-constraint]</a> below the bound in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The complete parameter verifier and its rational-logarithm interval certificate accompany the source. The proof requires a single feasible choice of parameters, with no assertion that this choice is optimal.

<a id="sec:foundations"></a>
### Rank and the basic tensor

Fix an arbitrary field $\mathbb F$. All tensor identities and linear maps below are over $\mathbb F$, and all logarithms are natural. A tensor is viewed as a trilinear form in three disjoint sets of variables. Its rank $\operatorname{R}(T)$ is the least number of products of three linear forms, one on each side, whose sum is $T$. A *restriction* substitutes linear forms separately on the three sides. Restrictions cannot increase rank. A direct sum uses disjoint variables, and a tensor product pairs the variable indices on each side; rank is subadditive under direct sum and submultiplicative under tensor product. These statements follow by applying the indicated operations to rank decompositions.

For positive integers $a,b,c$, write $$\langle a,b,c\rangle
   =\sum_{i=1}^{a}\sum_{j=1}^{b}\sum_{k=1}^{c}
       x_{ij}y_{jk}z_{ki}.$$ This is the tensor for multiplying an $a\times b$ matrix by a $b\times c$ matrix. Its *volume* is $abc$. Pairing the three matrix indices gives $$\langle a,b,c\rangle\otimes\langle a',b',c'\rangle
     \cong\langle aa',bb',cc'\rangle.$$ Cyclically permuting the three variable spaces replaces the parameters by $(b,c,a)$ or $(c,a,b)$ and preserves rank.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Exact rank and the arithmetic exponent

<a id="eq:rank-exponent"></a>
<a id="eq:square-rank-lower"></a>
Put $R_d=\operatorname{R}(\langle d,d,d\rangle)$ and $$\label{eq:rank-exponent}
 w=\inf_{d\geq2}\frac{\log R_d}{\log d},
 \qquad d\text{ an integer}.$$ The flattening that sends a $z$ coordinate to its coefficient bilinear form has rank $d^2$: the $d^2$ forms $\sum_jx_{ij}y_{jk}$ are linearly independent. A rank-one tensor has flattening rank at most one. Thus $d^2\leq R_d\leq d^3$ and $2\leq w\leq3$. In particular, $$\label{eq:square-rank-lower}
 R_d\geq d^w\qquad(d\geq1),$$ where $R_1=1$ handles the unit case.

Let $\omega$ denote the arithmetic exponent of square matrix multiplication over $\mathbb F$, counting scalar additions and multiplications. A fixed rank-$R_d$ decomposition gives a bilinear matrix multiplication identity with $R_d$ products. This identity remains valid on matrix blocks: every product has its $x$-linear factor before its $y$-linear factor, and comparison of coefficients proves the same identity for noncommuting blocks with central coefficients in $\mathbb F$. At size $d^k$ the resulting arithmetic cost satisfies $$C_k\leq R_d C_{k-1}+O_d(d^{2k}).$$ The implied constant may depend on the chosen decomposition. Since $R_d\geq d^2$, this gives $C_k=O_d(kR_d^k)$, including the boundary case $R_d=d^2$. Padding an arbitrary size to the next power of $d$ therefore gives cost $O_d(n^{\log_d R_d}\log(n+1))$. The logarithmic factor is absorbed by $n^\eta$ for every $\eta>0$; taking the infimum in <a href="#eq:rank-exponent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-exponent">[eq:rank-exponent]</a> proves <a id="eq:omega-rank-exponent"></a>$$\label{eq:omega-rank-exponent}
 \omega\leq w.$$

<a id="eq:square-rank-upper"></a>
We also need a uniform upper bound on exact square rank. Given $\varepsilon>0$, choose one integer $d\geq2$ with $\theta=\log_d R_d<w+\varepsilon$. For $h\geq1$, tensor powers and padding give $$\label{eq:square-rank-upper}
 \operatorname{R}(\langle h,h,h\rangle)
 \leq R_d^{\lceil\log_d h\rceil}
 \leq C_\varepsilon h^{w+\varepsilon},
 \qquad C_\varepsilon=R_d.$$ This argument uses one approximating dimension; it does not require the infimum defining $w$ to be attained.

We use the following identical-summand form of Schönhage’s asymptotic sum inequality (Schönhage 1981) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Schonhage1981](#ref-Schonhage1981)], with a proof from exact rank.

<a id="stmt-lem-direct-sum"></a>
<div id="lem:direct-sum" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:identical-direct-sum"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Direct sums of identical matrix tensors). *Let $s,a,b,c$ be positive integers. If $$\operatorname{R}\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s}\langle a,b,c\rangle\right)\leq R_*,$$ then $$\label{eq:identical-direct-sum}
 s(abc)^{w/3}\leq R_*.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-direct-sum"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-direct-sum</summary>

*Proof.* First consider $s$ copies of $\langle d,d,d\rangle$. When $s=1$, the assertion $sd^w\leq R_*$ is <a href="#eq:square-rank-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:square-rank-lower">[eq:square-rank-lower]</a>. When $d=1$, the direct sum is $\sum_{j=1}^s x_jy_jz_j$, whose flattening has rank $s$, so the assertion also holds. We may therefore assume $s,d\geq2$.

Fix $\varepsilon>0$ and the constant in <a href="#eq:square-rank-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:square-rank-upper">[eq:square-rank-upper]</a>. For all sufficiently large integers $k$, set $$h_k=\left\lfloor
       (s^k/C_\varepsilon)^{1/(w+\varepsilon)}
       \right\rfloor.$$ Then $h_k\geq2$, $\operatorname{R}(\langle h_k,h_k,h_k\rangle)\leq s^k$, and $$\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log h_k}{k}
      =\frac{\log s}{w+\varepsilon}.$$ The $k$th tensor power of the given direct sum is a direct sum of $s^k$ copies of $\langle d^k,d^k,d^k\rangle$, of rank at most $R_*^k$. A rank-$r$ decomposition of $\langle h_k,h_k,h_k\rangle$, with $r\leq s^k$, expresses it as a restriction of $r$ independent scalar products. Tensor this restriction with $\langle d^k,d^k,d^k\rangle$ and discard unused copies. It gives $\langle d^kh_k,d^kh_k,d^kh_k\rangle$ as a restriction of the available direct sum. Consequently $$R_*^k\geq(d^kh_k)^w.$$ Taking logarithms, dividing by $k$, and then taking $k\to\infty$ at fixed $\varepsilon$ yields $$\log R_*\geq w\log d+\frac{w}{w+\varepsilon}\log s.$$ Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ proves $sd^w\leq R_*$.

For the rectangular case, let $U=\bigoplus_{j=1}^s\langle a,b,c\rangle$ and tensor $U$ with its two cyclic rotations. Distributivity gives $s^3$ independent copies of $$\langle a,b,c\rangle\otimes\langle b,c,a\rangle
       \otimes\langle c,a,b\rangle
       \cong\langle abc,abc,abc\rangle,$$ and the product has rank at most $R_*^3$. The square case gives $s^3(abc)^w\leq R_*^3$, whose cube root is <a href="#eq:identical-direct-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:identical-direct-sum">[eq:identical-direct-sum]</a>. The same proof covers $abc=1$ through the unit square case. ◻

</details>

The hypothesis in Lemma <a href="#lem:direct-sum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:direct-sum">2</a> specifies one common *oriented* triple $(a,b,c)$ for all summands. In the construction below, exact history counts and common terminal type restrictions will provide these identical dimensions.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### An integer coefficient construction

The relation between approximate bilinear identities and exact algorithms was studied by Bini (Bini 1980) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Bini1980](#ref-Bini1980)]. Here a direct coefficient calculation gives an exact rank bound over the original field, including when that field is finite.

For an integer $q\geq1$, define the symmetric Coppersmith–Winograd tensor (Coppersmith and Winograd 1990) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[CoppersmithWinograd1990](#ref-CoppersmithWinograd1990)] $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathrm{CW}_q={}&\sum_{i=1}^q
  (x_0y_iz_i+x_iy_0z_i+x_iy_iz_0)\\
 &+x_{q+1}y_0z_0+x_0y_{q+1}z_0+x_0y_0z_{q+1}.
\end{aligned}$$ We use $q=5$. The following exact-rank estimate makes the field independence and the polynomial overhead explicit.

<a id="stmt-lem-cw-rank"></a>
<div id="lem:cw-rank" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cw-power-rank"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Rank bound for powers of the basic tensor). *Over every field and for every integer $m\geq0$, $$\label{eq:cw-power-rank}
 \operatorname{R}(\mathrm{CW}_q^{\otimes m})
      \leq(q+2)^m\binom{3m+2}{2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cw-rank"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cw-rank</summary>

<a id="eq:cw-laurent"></a>
<a id="eq:cw-constant-power"></a>
*Proof.* We use the Laurent-polynomial identity of Coppersmith and Winograd (Coppersmith and Winograd 1990, sec. 7, equation (10)) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[CoppersmithWinograd1990](#ref-CoppersmithWinograd1990)] over the integers, with a formal variable $t$. Set $X_1=\sum_{i=1}^q x_i$, $Y_1=\sum_{i=1}^q y_i$, and $Z_1=\sum_{i=1}^q z_i$, and consider $$\begin{aligned}
 P(t)={}&t^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^q
        (x_0+tx_i)(y_0+ty_i)(z_0+tz_i)\notag\\
 &-t^{-3}(x_0+t^2X_1)(y_0+t^2Y_1)(z_0+t^2Z_1)\notag\\
 &+(t^{-3}-qt^{-2})
        (x_0+t^3x_{q+1})(y_0+t^3y_{q+1})(z_0+t^3z_{q+1}).
        \label{eq:cw-laurent}
\end{aligned}$$ The only possible negative degrees are $-3,-2,-1$. Their coefficients are, respectively, $$(-1+1)x_0y_0z_0,\qquad
 (q-q)x_0y_0z_0,\qquad
 (1-1)(X_1y_0z_0+x_0Y_1z_0+x_0y_0Z_1).$$ The degree-zero coefficient is precisely $\mathrm{CW}_q$. Thus $P(t)=\mathrm{CW}_q+tQ(t)$ for a polynomial $Q$ with integer coefficients. In particular, $$\label{eq:cw-constant-power}
 [t^0]P(t)^{\otimes m}=\mathrm{CW}_q^{\otimes m}.$$ These coefficient identities remain valid after reduction to any field.

Regard <a href="#eq:cw-laurent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cw-laurent">[eq:cw-laurent]</a> as $q+2$ products of three Laurent-polynomial linear forms, absorbing the scalar prefactor of each product into its $x$ form. In one tensor position the possible degrees on the $x$ side lie, respectively, in $$\{-2,-1\},\qquad\{-3,-1\},\qquad\{-3,-2,0,1\}.$$ The last set includes both terms of $t^{-3}-qt^{-2}$ within a single linear form. On each of the other two sides the degrees lie between $0$ and $3$.

Expanding $P(t)^{\otimes m}$ therefore gives $(q+2)^m$ products, each of the form $A(t)B(t)C(t)$ with $$A(t)=\sum_{a=-3m}^{m}A_at^a,\qquad
 B(t)=\sum_{b=0}^{3m}B_bt^b,\qquad
 C(t)=\sum_{c=0}^{3m}C_ct^c.$$ Here each coefficient is one linear form in the corresponding tensor-product variables. The constant coefficient of this product is $$\sum_{\substack{b,c\geq0\\b+c\leq3m}}
       A_{-b-c}B_bC_c,$$ a sum of at most $\binom{3m+2}{2}$ rank-one tensors. Summing over the $(q+2)^m$ choices and using <a href="#eq:cw-constant-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cw-constant-power">[eq:cw-constant-power]</a> proves <a href="#eq:cw-power-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cw-power-rank">[eq:cw-power-rank]</a>. For $m=0$, both sides equal $1$ under the empty tensor product convention. Only coefficient addition and multiplication were used; no interpolation or field division is needed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Strands and shapes

Give the label $0$ weight $0$, the labels $1,\ldots,q$ weight $1$, and the label $q+1$ weight $2$. Every term of $\mathrm{CW}_q$ has total weight $2$. A *strand of length $\ell$* is a copy of $\mathrm{CW}_q^{\otimes\ell}$; its variable indices are words of length $\ell$. For a triple $g=(g_0,g_1,g_2)$ of nonnegative integers with $g_0+g_1+g_2=2\ell$, let $T_{\ell,g}$ be the restriction in which the weight sums on the three sides are $g_0,g_1,g_2$. The coordinates of $g$ refer to sides, whereas the weights $0,1,2$ refer to individual labels. A shape is *positive* if all three coordinates are positive. When $\ell$ is even, splitting a strand into its two consecutive halves gives child shapes $u$ and $g-u$, where $0\leq u\leq g$ coordinatewise and $\sum_i u_i=\ell$.

A zero coordinate gives an especially simple terminal tensor. Suppose $g_0=0$. The $x$ word is then forced to be the all-zero word. In each position a label on the $y$ side uniquely determines the $z$ label: $0$ and $q+1$ are exchanged, and every label $i\in\{1,\ldots,q\}$ is fixed. Thus the other two sides are matched bijectively, and $T_{\ell,g}$ is $\langle1,1,M\rangle$, where $$M=[z^{g_1}](1+qz+z^2)^\ell.$$ For $g_1=0$ or $g_2=0$ the corresponding orientations are $\langle M,1,1\rangle$ or $\langle1,M,1\rangle$. Restricting the matched words by corresponding type conditions on their two sides preserves this form, with $M$ replaced by the number of allowed matched pairs; an empty matching gives the zero tensor. This observation will supply the terminal volume counts.

<a id="sec:joint"></a>
### A joint extraction at several depths

The constituent-stage analysis of (Alman et al. 2026, Theorem 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[MoreAsymmetry2026](#ref-MoreAsymmetry2026)] combines differently prescribed populations at a common depth. The extraction below also permits populations at different depths to share one hashing step, provided their priority orders agree on the physical tensor sides. Population data, strand lengths, and the constant sums defining their coarse supports may differ. The successive compatibility checks follow (Alman et al. 2026, sec. 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[MoreAsymmetry2026](#ref-MoreAsymmetry2026)], building on the asymmetric hashing and interface-tensor methods of (Duan, Wu, and Zhou 2023; Vassilevska Williams et al. 2023) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[DuanWuZhou2023](#ref-DuanWuZhou2023), [VXXZ2023](#ref-VXXZ2023)]. We specify the intended output first, then derive the three capacities that govern its extraction yield.

For a probability vector $p$ on a finite alphabet, write $H(p)=-\sum_a p(a)\log p(a)$, with $0\log0=0$. A word has *type* $p$ if its empirical distribution is exactly $p$; a type window of width $\theta$ means coordinatewise distance at most $\theta$. All alphabets, populations, and positive population masses in this section are fixed as $N$ tends to infinity.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The data of a joint step

Index the populations by a finite set $\mathcal R$. Population $r$ has $m_r=c_rN$ copies of a fixed strand tensor $F_r$ with finite variable sets, where $c_r>0$ is rational. On each side its variables are partitioned into coarse blocks indexed by $\{0,\ldots,16\}$. Every monomial of $F_r$ has its triple of coarse indices in an ambient support $\Omega_r$, whose elements $u=(u_X,u_Y,u_Z)$ satisfy <a id="eq:coarse-sum"></a>$$\label{eq:coarse-sum}
 u_X+u_Y+u_Z=s_r.$$ Here $X,Y,Z$ is one common ordering of the physical sides. In the product $\bigotimes_r F_r^{\otimes m_r}$, a coarse block is specified by a sequence of indices, one for every strand. A fine side variable is a complete basis variable of the product input; its coarse block records the coarse index at every strand position. The fixed strand tensor in a population is repeated identically at all its positions. Populations with different histories or physical placements are kept distinct.

Choose a rational law $p_r$ on $\Omega_r$ that maximizes entropy among all laws on $\Omega_r$ with its three marginals. The support of $p_r$ may be smaller than $\Omega_r$. There are two kinds of population.

1.  An *ordinary population* delivers the full tensor block at each selected triple $u$, without further output type tests. The blocks may themselves be tensor products of child strands. This includes extraction by whole-strand shape and the final split into length-one strands.

2.  A *sharing population* consists of copies of $T_{\ell,g}$, with even $\ell$ and $\sum_Wg_W=2\ell$. Its coarse index $u_W$ is the weight sum in the left half, so $$\Omega_r=\{u:0\le u\le g,\ \textstyle\sum_Wu_W=\ell\},
     \qquad T_{\ell,g}|_u=T_{\ell/2,u}\otimes T_{\ell/2,g-u}.$$ Assume $p_r(u)=p_r(g-u)$. On each side $W$, a statistic of the full half-strand variable takes values in a fixed finite alphabet $\mathcal A_{r,W}$. A map $\rho_W$ from this alphabet to weights determines the half weight sum. For every half-shape $u$ with $p_r(u)>0$, prescribe a rational law $\nu_{r,u,W}$ supported on $\rho_W^{-1}(u_W)$.

    When a half-shape has a zero coordinate, the full variables on the other two sides match by complementation. Require that their statistics also match by a fixed bijection and that their prescribed laws are its pushforwards. A side having a unique possible variable has its corresponding point-mass prescription. These requirements apply as well when two coordinates vanish.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Ideal outputs and inherited masks.

A *target triple* $e$ is a triple of coarse sequences having exact joint type $p_r$ in each population. Start with the tensor block at $e$. In every sharing population, impose a positive type window around $\nu_{r,u,W}$ separately in every class of positions of given $(r,u,\text{half})$, on each side. The right-half prescription in the class indexed by $u$ is $\nu_{r,g-u,W}$. Leave ordinary blocks unrestricted. The resulting tensor product is the *ideal output* $Q_e$. Target triples have the same class sizes, so their ideal outputs are isomorphic by position permutations preserving the physical sides; denote a canonical such tensor by $Q_N$. A fine side variable in a coarse block of $e$ is *useful for $e$* if it satisfies all these output windows; ordinary populations contribute no further condition. Thus usefulness specifies the variables of the ideal tensor. Additional input or separation tests may delete some of them.

<a id="eq:joint-pair-law"></a>
The child windows constrain the two halves separately. Inherited conditions can also constrain how their statistics are paired at parent positions. For a sharing population, suppress $r$ temporarily and define the mixture law, omitting all zero-mass shapes as we do below: $$\label{eq:joint-pair-law}
 D_W=\sum_{u\in\Omega_r}p(u)\,
       \nu_{u,W}\otimes\nu_{g-u,W}.$$ This is the expected pair law when, within each shape class, the left and right statistics have their prescribed laws and are independently arranged.

An input mask is always a deletion of individual side variables. For sharing populations we allow finitely many empirical tests on the ordered pair of half statistics, or a fixed function of that pair. Their centers must be the law $D_{r,W}$ in <a href="#eq:joint-pair-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:joint-pair-law">[eq:joint-pair-law]</a>, or its corresponding pushforward, and their widths must be fixed positive numbers. Ordinary input masks must already follow from the exact target coarse types and the specified ideal blocks. No other inherited masks are assumed. All masks are imposed separately in the populations just defined. A mask on a larger received class can instead be enforced separately, with tighter widths and the same center, on any fixed subdivision; the resulting partwise restrictions imply the original one.

Choose output widths small enough that replacing each prescribed child law by any law in its window leaves the expected ordered-pair law strictly inside every inherited window. This condition has a uniform positive margin: for coordinatewise child errors at most $\theta$, a product-law coordinate changes by at most $2\theta$, and all relevant collections are finite. The same margin will be required for the additional pair tests introduced in the proof.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### Compatibility groups and capacities.

The extraction will first separate targets by their $X$ coarse blocks and impose the $X$ output windows. It then separates $Y$ variables, imposes their output windows, and finally separates $Z$ variables. The later-side separation tests use only those individual shape types that matching forces from an earlier side. Define their designated shapes by <a id="eq:designated-compatibility"></a>$$\label{eq:designated-compatibility}
 \mathcal I_Y=\{u\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}p:u_Z=0\},\qquad
 \mathcal I_Z=\{u\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}p:u_Xu_Y=0\}.$$ A zero $Z$ coordinate lets matching transfer the $X$-side output type condition to $Y$. For $Z$, a zero $Y$ coordinate transfers the condition from $X$; a zero $X$ coordinate instead uses $Y$, after its output windows have been imposed. If both $X$ and $Y$ coordinates vanish, the $Z$ variable is forced. For the remaining shapes with a given $W$ coordinate, the pair law $D_W$ determines only an aggregate statistic law after the designated contributions are removed. We impose no individual compatibility test on these shapes. The exact predicate and its tolerances are given in the incidence count below. For $W\in\{Y,Z\}$, the partition $\mathcal G_W$ consists of the individual groups $\{u\}$ for $u\in\mathcal I_W$, and the residual groups $\{u\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}p\setminus\mathcal I_W:u_W=i\}$. Empty groups are omitted. A residual group is treated as residual even when it contains just one shape. Put $p(G)=\sum_{u\in G}p(u)$ and define <a id="eq:joint-group-entropy"></a>$$\label{eq:joint-group-entropy}
 J_W=\sum_{G\in\mathcal G_W}p(G)
 H\left(\frac{\sum_{u\in G}p(u)\nu_{u,W}}{p(G)}\right),
 \qquad W\in\{Y,Z\}.$$ The entropy $J_W$ measures the choices for one half-statistic word within these groups. Counting both halves gives the cost $2J_W$; comparison with the pair-word entropy $H(D_W)$ gives the saving that controls the number of compatible targets. Accordingly, define the sharing capacities by <a id="eq:sharing-capacities"></a>$$\label{eq:sharing-capacities}
 C_{r,X}=H(p_{r,X}),\qquad
 C_{r,Y}=H(D_{r,Y})-2J_{r,Y},\qquad
 C_{r,Z}=H(D_{r,Z})-2J_{r,Z}.$$ Only the law of half-shapes is assumed invariant under $u\mapsto g-u$; the two prescriptions $\nu_{u,W}$ and $\nu_{g-u,W}$ may differ.

For an ordinary population no compatibility restriction is needed, and the capacities are simply $$C_{r,X}=H(p_{r,X}),\qquad C_{r,Y}=H(p_{r,Y}),\qquad
 C_{r,Z}=H(p_{r,Z}).$$ The counting arguments below give a common interpretation: each $C_{r,W}$ is the entropy saving in the bound on competitors at side $W$. The joint step adds these savings over populations before taking their minimum.

<a id="stmt-thm-joint-step"></a>
<div id="thm:joint-step" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4** (Heterogeneous joint step). *Let $B_N$ be the restriction of $\bigotimes_r F_r^{\otimes m_r}$ by the inherited masks just specified, with the population data above. Set <a id="eq:joint-totals"></a>$$\label{eq:joint-totals}
 A_{\rm amb}=\sum_{r\in\mathcal R}c_rH(p_r),\qquad
 C_W=\sum_{r\in\mathcal R}c_rC_{r,W},\qquad
 E=\min\{C_X,C_Y,C_Z\}.$$ If $E>0$, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ one can choose positive output widths, as small as desired, such that for all sufficiently large $N$ divisible by the fixed type denominators, a direct sum of $\exp(o(N))$ independent copies of $B_N$ has a linear restriction to at least $\lfloor\exp(N(E-\varepsilon))\rfloor$ independent copies of $Q_N$.*

*The choices of populations, rational laws, positive widths, and divisibility requirements precede the limit $N\to\infty$. In particular, the assertion does not require common strand lengths.*

</div>

We first bound hash survival and assignment collisions. The sequential $X,Y,Z$ tests then put surviving monomials in a direct sum of variable restrictions of the ideal outputs. Section <a href="#sec:recovery" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:recovery">4</a> completes the proof by bounding the missing fraction on every full-variable orbit and restoring the ideal outputs from $\exp(o(N))$ input copies.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Entropy and a common modular hash

For a fixed alphabet $\mathcal A$, the number of words of type $p$ is <a id="eq:type-count"></a>$$\label{eq:type-count}
 \frac{m!}{\prod_a(mp(a))!}
   =\exp\bigl(mH(p)+O(\log(m+2))\bigr).$$ Indeed, comparing the sum $\sum_{j=1}^n\log j$ with its integral gives $\log(n!)=n\log n-n+O(\log(n+2))$. There are at most $(m+1)^{|\mathcal A|}$ possible types. Both bounds are uniform on each fixed alphabet, including types with zero coordinates.

One convenient way to meet the entropy hypothesis is to use positive weights $a_i,b_j,c_k$ and set $$p(i,j,k)=Z^{-1}a_i b_j c_k\quad ((i,j,k)\in\Omega_r).$$ For any competing law $q$ with the same marginals, $-\sum q\log p=-\sum p\log p=H(p)$, because $\log p$ is a sum of three one-coordinate functions and a constant. Further, $$\sum_{q(u)>0}q(u)\log\frac{p(u)}{q(u)}
 \le \sum_{q(u)>0}(p(u)-q(u))\le0,$$ by $\log t\le t-1$. Thus $H(q)\le H(p)$. Positive rational weights give rational laws with this same property. When the marginals specify a unique law, maximality also holds, including boundary laws with zero probabilities.

<a id="eq:target-count"></a>
<a id="eq:ambient-x-degree"></a>
First keep, on each side, just the coarse sequences having marginal type $p_{r,W}$ in every population. Call a triple satisfying these three filters and the support conditions *ambient*. The total number of target triples is $$\label{eq:target-count}
 |\mathcal T_N|=\exp\bigl(NA_{\rm amb}+O(\log N)\bigr).$$ For a fixed $X$ coarse sequence, each possible ambient joint type $q_r$ has the prescribed marginals. Its number of completions in population $r$ is $$\frac{\prod_i(mp_{r,X}(i))!}{\prod_u(mq_r(u))!}
 =\exp\bigl(m(H(q_r)-H(p_{r,X}))+O(\log N)\bigr).$$ Summing over the polynomially many types and using maximality yields the degree bound $$\label{eq:ambient-x-degree}
 \#\{\text{ambient triples at a fixed $X$ block}\}
 \le \exp\bigl(N(A_{\rm amb}-C_X)+O(\log N)\bigr).$$ Here and below the coarse counts ignore all fine masks, thereby bounding every interaction that could actually occur.

<a id="eq:hash-size"></a>
<a id="eq:ap-free-size"></a>
Fix a small slack $\delta>0$. Since $C_X\le A_{\rm amb}$ and $E\le C_X$, choose a power $M=37^k$ with $$\label{eq:hash-size}
 \log M=N(A_{\rm amb}-E+\delta)+O(1).$$ There is a set $U\subset\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z$ with $$\label{eq:ap-free-size}
 \log|U|=\log M-O(\sqrt{\log M})$$ and no nonconstant three-term arithmetic progression. Here is a version of Behrend’s construction (Behrend 1946) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Behrend1946](#ref-Behrend1946)] that also rules out modular wraparound. Put $h=\lfloor\sqrt{\log M}\rfloor$, $b=\lfloor M^{1/h}/2\rfloor$, and encode $d\in\{0,\ldots,b-1\}^h$ as $n(d)=\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}d_j(2b)^j$. Choose a largest class with fixed $\sum_jd_j^2$. Every encoded integer is less than $(2b)^h/2\le M/2$, so a modular progression among them is an integer progression. In $n(d)+n(e)=2n(f)$ all digit sums are at most $2b-2$, so there is no carry and $d+e=2f$. Equal squared norms now give $\|d-e\|^2=2\|d\|^2+2\|e\|^2-\|d+e\|^2=0$. There are at most $1+h(b-1)^2$ norm classes, whence $|U|\ge b^h/(1+hb^2)$. The inequalities $b\ge M^{1/h}/4$ and $b\le M^{1/h}$ imply <a href="#eq:ap-free-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ap-free-size">[eq:ap-free-size]</a>.

Concatenate all $d=\sum_r m_r$ strand positions to form coarse words $I,J,K$. Let $S$ have entry $s_r$ at positions in population $r$. Every ambient triple satisfies $I+J+K=S$, even though the entries of $S$ can vary. Choose independent uniform offsets $a,b'$ and a uniform vector $v$ over $\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z$, and set <a id="eq:joint-hashes"></a>$$\label{eq:joint-hashes}
 x(I)=a+\langle v,I\rangle,\quad
 y(J)=b'+\langle v,J\rangle,\quad
 z(K)=\frac{a+b'+\langle v,S-K\rangle}{2}.$$ Division by $2$ is defined since $M$ is odd. Keep only coarse blocks whose hashes lie in $U$. Since $x+y=2z$, a surviving triple has $x=y=z$.

<a id="eq:hash-survival"></a>
<a id="eq:hash-collision"></a>
For a fixed ambient triple $e$, let $S_e$ be its full hash-survival event. For every $v$ and $t\in U$ there is exactly one offset pair giving $x=y=z=t$, so $$\label{eq:hash-survival}
 \Pr(S_e)=h_M:=\frac{|U|}{M^2}.$$ Conditional on $S_e$, $(v,t)$ is uniform on $(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^d\times U$. A second triple with differences $(\Delta I,\Delta J,\Delta K)$ has hashes $$t+\langle v,\Delta I\rangle,\quad
 t+\langle v,\Delta J\rangle,\quad
 t-\tfrac12\langle v,\Delta K\rangle.$$ If it shares $X$ with $e$, survival is equivalent to $\langle v,\Delta J\rangle=0$; if it shares $Y$ or $Z$, it is equivalent to $\langle v,\Delta I\rangle=0$. In the shared-$Z$ case use $\Delta J=-\Delta I$. Distinct triples cannot share two blocks, by their common sum $S$. Thus the tested difference vector has a nonzero coordinate of absolute value at most $16$, which is a unit modulo $37^k$. Conditioning on the other coordinates of $v$ shows, in all three cases, that $$\label{eq:hash-collision}
 \Pr(S_{e'}\mid S_e)=\frac1M
 \quad\text{for distinct triples sharing a block}.$$ No further hash-dependent event is included in this conditioning.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The finite compatibility count

In a sharing population, prefilter side $W\in\{Y,Z\}$ by requiring its empirical ordered-pair law to be within width $\eta$ of $D_W$. A fine side variable is *compatible* with a target triple if its statistic type is within width $\chi$ of the prescribed law in every designated shape class, separately on the two halves. On the left these are the positions with $u\in\mathcal I_W$, using $\nu_{u,W}$; on the right they are the positions with $g-u\in\mathcal I_W$, using $\nu_{g-u,W}$. No individual compatibility test is imposed on a residual group, including a residual group with one shape. The positions of each tested class are read from the target triple. Ordinary populations impose no compatibility test. A variable for the whole product is compatible exactly when it is compatible in every population.

<a id="stmt-lem-compatibility-count"></a>
<div id="lem:compatibility-count" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:compatibility-degree"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Compatibility incidence count). *For fixed population data, there is a function $\xi(\eta,\chi)\to0$ as $(\eta,\chi)\to(0,0)$ such that any fine side variable passing the pair filters is compatible with at most $$\label{eq:compatibility-degree}
 \exp\bigl(N(A_{\rm amb}-C_W+\xi(\eta,\chi))
                    +O(\log N)\bigr),\qquad W\in\{Y,Z\},$$ target triples containing its coarse block, before hashing. The estimate is uniform in the variable and the target arrangement.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-compatibility-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compatibility-count</summary>

<a id="eq:incidence-identity"></a>
*Proof.* Consider first one sharing population of size $m$, and fix the coarse $W$ word $k=(k_1,\ldots,k_m)$. If $k$ does not have type $p_W$, no target contains it and the bound is immediate. Assume henceforth that it has this type, and let $\mathcal C$ be the set of shape words $(u_1,\ldots,u_m)$ of type $p$ with $u_{j,W}=k_j$. Such a word determines a complete coarse triple. If the given fine variable has pair-statistic word $z=((a_j,b_j))_{j=1}^m$ and empirical law $D'$, let $\mathcal B$ be its orbit under $$\Gamma_k=\prod_i\operatorname{Sym}\{j:k_j=i\}.$$ This group acts transitively on $\mathcal B$ by definition and on $\mathcal C$ because all shape counts within every coarse class are fixed. Compatibility is unchanged by applying the same permutation to a candidate and a statistic word. Hence its incidence degrees $a=\#\{z\in\mathcal B:z\text{ compatible with }c\}$ and $b=\#\{c\in\mathcal C:z\text{ compatible with }c\}$ do not depend on $c$ and $z$, respectively. Counting incidences gives the exact finite identity $$\label{eq:incidence-identity}
 |\mathcal C|a=|\mathcal B|b.$$ Since the statistic determines the half weight, $$\begin{aligned}
 |\mathcal C|&=\frac{\prod_i(mp_W(i))!}{\prod_u(mp(u))!},
 &\log|\mathcal C|&=m(H(p)-H(p_W))+O(\log(m+2)),\\
 |\mathcal B|&=\frac{\prod_i(mp_W(i))!}{\prod_{a,b}(mD'(a,b))!},
 &\log|\mathcal B|&=m(H(D')-H(p_W))+O(\log(m+2)).
\end{aligned}$$

To bound $a$, now fix a candidate $c\in\mathcal C$. This fixes the position set of every left and right compatibility group. On the left, compatibility specifies the law within each designated group to accuracy $\chi$. The row sums of $D'$ give the total count of each statistic among positions with each coarse value, since $\rho_W(a_j)=k_j$. Subtracting the designated-group counts leaves, in the remaining group with coarse value $i$, the law $$\frac{\sum_{u\in G}p(u)\nu_{u,W}}{p(G)}$$ up to an error tending uniformly to zero with $\eta$ and $\chi$. More explicitly, each coordinate error is at most $(|\mathcal A_{r,W}|\eta+\chi)/p(G)$: summing a row of $D'$ costs at most $|\mathcal A_{r,W}|\eta$, and the weighted errors of the subtracted designated laws total at most $\chi$. Only fixed positive numbers $p(G)$ are divided by. A product of multinomial counts over these fixed position sets, and then a sum over polynomially many types, bounds the number of left-statistic assignments by $\exp(m(J_W+o_{\eta,\chi}(1))+O(\log(m+2)))$.

For the right half, use the column sums of $D'$. Its coarse value is $g_W-k_j$, so those sums determine precisely the analogous aggregate counts. The right half-shape distribution is $p(g-u)=p(u)$, giving the same entropy $J_W$, with the actual right prescription $\nu_{g-u,W}$. Count the two halves independently; discarding the requirement that they reassemble the exact pair law $D'$ can only enlarge the count. We obtain $$a\le\exp\bigl(m(2J_W+o_{\eta,\chi}(1))+O(\log(m+2))\bigr).$$ There is no factor for choosing group locations: those locations were fixed with $c$. By <a href="#eq:incidence-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:incidence-identity">[eq:incidence-identity]</a> and uniform continuity of entropy on a finite simplex, $$b\le\exp\bigl(m(H(p)-H(D_W)+2J_W+o_{\eta,\chi}(1))
                         +O(\log(m+2))\bigr).$$ For an ordinary population all candidates are compatible, and their number is $\exp(m(H(p)-H(p_W))+O(\log(m+2)))$ directly. The candidate sets, permutation groups, and compatibility conditions factor over populations. Multiplying their bounds proves <a href="#eq:compatibility-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compatibility-degree">[eq:compatibility-degree]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Sequential assignment by variable deletion

Choose $\eta,\chi>0$ sufficiently small that the error in Lemma <a href="#lem:compatibility-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compatibility-count">5</a> is below $\delta/4$. Next choose output widths $\theta>0$ smaller than $\chi$ and small enough for the positive margins required by all inherited and pair tests. Complementary prescribed laws ensure that usefulness of one side transfers to the compatibility window on a matching side. Every choice here is fixed before $N$ grows.

First delete an $X$ coarse block unless it belongs to exactly one ambient hash survivor and that survivor is a target triple. Call the retained triples *eligible*. Freeze this list for all subsequent tests. By <a href="#eq:ambient-x-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ambient-x-degree">[eq:ambient-x-degree]</a>, <a href="#eq:hash-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hash-size">[eq:hash-size]</a>, and <a href="#eq:hash-collision" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hash-collision">[eq:hash-collision]</a>, a target triple $e$ fails this test, conditional only on $S_e$, with probability at most $\exp(-\delta N+O(\log N))$. Each retained $X$ block now has an unambiguous assignment. In that block retain only the fine variables useful for its assigned triple, that is, those satisfying its output windows.

On $Y$, apply its pair filters and delete every fine variable having other than exactly one compatible eligible triple containing its coarse block. Assign it to that triple and impose usefulness for that assignment. Apply the same procedure to $Z$, using the designated classes $\mathcal I_Z$. Include all inherited masks as side-variable deletions; their application does not change the frozen eligible list or the definitions of the compatible lists.

We verify that these operations produce a direct sum, rather than merely a list of proposed components. Take an actual monomial surviving every deletion, and let $e$ be its ambient coarse triple. Its $X$ block survived, so $e$ is that block’s unique eligible triple and its $X$ variable is useful for $e$. In every sharing half with $u_Z=0$, the $Y$ statistics are complementary to the $X$ statistics. Thus the actual $Y$ variable is compatible with $e$; deterministic sides satisfy their point-mass tests as well. The $Y$ uniqueness test therefore assigns this variable to $e$. Its usefulness is now known for the actual triple.

For $Z$, a half with $u_Y=0$ transfers the required statistics from $X$. A half with $u_X=0$ and $u_Y>0$ transfers them from the already useful $Y$ variable. With both $u_X=u_Y=0$, the remaining variable is forced. These cases prove compatibility with $e$ on every designated shape class in $\mathcal I_Z$. Its uniqueness test then assigns the actual $Z$ variable to $e$ as well. Ordinary populations add no compatibility conditions in either argument. All three variables of every retained monomial therefore have the same assignment.

Different assignments share no retained fine variable on any side. Within assignment $e$, usefulness restricts precisely to $Q_e$; all other tests delete individual variables of $Q_e$. Consequently the surviving tensor is a direct sum of tensors $Q_e^{\rm hole}$, each obtained from its ideal $Q_e$ solely by variable deletion. This statement concerns complete variables of the population product; sharing a local coordinate factor between two complete variables does not identify those variables.

<a id="eq:joint-conditional-loss"></a>
Here is the probability estimate needed to repair these holes. For a fixed $e$ and a fixed variable $v$ of $Q_e$ passing all its deterministic inherited and pair masks, own-triple compatibility and usefulness hold. Let $R_{e,v}$ be the union of the $X$ eligibility-failure event and, when $v$ is on side $Y$ or $Z$, the events that another compatible target triple sharing its coarse block survives the hash. Every random deletion of $v$ is contained in $R_{e,v}$. Bound those competitors among *all* target triples before eligibility and other deletions. Lemma <a href="#lem:compatibility-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compatibility-count">5</a> and <a href="#eq:hash-collision" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hash-collision">[eq:hash-collision]</a> then give, for some constant $c>0$, $$\label{eq:joint-conditional-loss}
 \Pr(R_{e,v}\mid S_e)
 \le \exp(-cN),$$ uniformly in $e$ and the passing variable, for all large $N$. Indeed, the exponent for side $W$ is at most $N(E-C_W-\delta+\xi)+O(\log N)$ and $C_W\ge E$. This estimate is conditioned only on $S_e$, never on eligibility or on passing a hash-dependent test.

For reference in the recovery argument, let $G_e$ act by independent position permutations in every ideal child class $(r,u,\text{half})$, simultaneously on all three sides. In ordinary whole-strand blocks use their position permutations; for a factorized ordinary block one may also permute its child factors independently. These are automorphisms of $Q_e$. The full variable alphabets in its finitely many classes are fixed, so $G_e$ has only polynomially many orbits of variables. Lemma <a href="#lem:orbit-masks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:orbit-masks">6</a> supplies the uniform deterministic mask bound on these orbits. The completion of Theorem <a href="#thm:joint-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:joint-step">4</a> in Section <a href="#sec:recovery" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:recovery">4</a> combines that bound with <a href="#eq:joint-conditional-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:joint-conditional-loss">[eq:joint-conditional-loss]</a>, selects exponentially many good assignments using <a href="#eq:target-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-count">[eq:target-count]</a> and <a href="#eq:hash-survival" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hash-survival">[eq:hash-survival]</a>, and applies Lemma <a href="#lem:hole-repair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hole-repair">7</a> to reconstruct their ideal outputs.

<a id="sec:recovery"></a>
### Recovering the ideal outputs and composing extractions

The assignment procedure in Section <a href="#sec:joint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:joint">3</a> leaves a direct sum of variable restrictions of the intended outputs. We now select sufficiently many of these outputs and recover them exactly. Two different probability spaces occur: permutations of an ideal output control inherited masks, whereas the random hash controls assignment collisions. Keeping these spaces separate is essential when a received population already has type restrictions. Recovery from deletions on all three sides appears in (Vassilevska Williams et al. 2023, sec. 7, especially Property 7.1 and Theorem 7.2) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[VXXZ2023](#ref-VXXZ2023)]; its interface-tensor consequence is Corollary 4.2 there.
